115 年 8 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 9.4%，較上年
同月增 41.0%(按新臺幣計算增 52.6%)；累計 1 至 8 月出口 5,743.4
億美元，較上年同期增 44.2%(按新臺幣計算增 46.5%)。 
（二）進口 
8 月進口 601.0 億美元，為歷年單月第 3 高，較上月增 3.4%，較上
年同月增 44.3%(按新臺幣計算增 56.2%)；累 計1 至 8 月進口 4,374.3
億美元，較上年同期增 40.4%(按新臺幣計算增 42.4%)。 
（三）出入超 
8 月出超 223.0 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 55.2 億美元；
累計 1 至 8 月出超 1,369.1 億美元，較上年同期增 500.5 億美元。

### Page 2

二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、 高效能運算及雲端服務等應用持續擴增，相關
產品出貨維持強勁， 加上價格攀升效 應，8月出口較上年同月增 239.7
億美元(+41.0%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 電子零組件出口 322.2 億美元(歷年單月新高)， 較上年同月增118.3
億美元(+58.0%)，其中積體電路增 115.5 億美元(+60.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+29.9%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.7 億美元(+24.9%)。 
2. 資通與視聽產品出口 331.0 億美元，較上年同月增 97.6 億美元
(+41.8%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增68.7 億美元(+40.4%)、儲存媒
體增 19.1 億美元(+2.4 倍)、 交換器及路由器增9.7 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 礦產品出口 20.0 億美元，較上年同月增 8.3 億美元(+70.2%)，其
中石油煉製品增 7.8 億美元(+74.3%)。 
4. 基本金屬及其製品出口 29.6 億美元，較上年同月增 5.4 億美元
(+22.4%)，其中銅及其製品增 6.1 億美元(+1.5 倍)，鋼鐵及其製品
減 1.1 億美元(-8.7%)。 
5. 機械出口 26.8 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+15.2%)，其中
生產半導體等機械增 1.9 億美元(+42.1%)、未列名具特殊功能機械
增 0.4 億美元(+39.0%)。

累計 1 至 8 月出口較上年同期增 1,759.8 億美元(+44.2%)，以資通與
視聽產品增 1,023.7 億美元(+68.5%)、電子零組件增 629.5 億美元
(+45.8%)增加較多。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路及資通產
品進口維持強勁，加上輸入物價明顯上漲，8 月總進口較上年同月增
184.5 億美元(+44.3%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口408.4 億美元， 較上年同月增129.4億美元(+46.4%)，
其中電子零組件增 82.5 億美元(+62.6%)、礦產品增 16.9 億美元
(+38.4%)、電腦之零附件增 14.6 億美元(+84.4%)、基本金屬增 4.5
億美元(+21.8%)、塑化原料增 4.0 億美元(+16.2%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 130.6 億美元， 較上年同月增 38.4 億美元(+41.6%)，
其中資通設備增 27.2 億美元(+1.2 倍)、半導體設備增 2.8 億美元
(+7.3%)、交通運輸設備增 2.8 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 55.7 億美元，較上年同月增 16.0 億美元(+40.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增12.1 億美元(+3.7 倍)、藥粧用品增 1.2
億美元(+17.4%)、手機增 1.1 億美元(+78.1%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月對陸港、日本出口創新高，對美國、東協為單月第 3 高 
1. 對美國出口 236.7 億美元，較上年同月增 40.6 億美元(+20.7%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 23.0 億美元(+14.6%)、電子零組件增 11.6
億美元(+1.5 倍)、電機產品增 1.7 億美元(+30.1%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 211.5 億美元，較上年同月增 59.7 億美元
(+39.3%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+45.6%)、資通與視聽
產品增 6.7 億美元(+42.9%)、 基本金屬及其製品增4.6 億美元(+1.2
倍)。 
3. 對東協出口 151.3 億美元，較上年同月增 44.9 億美元(+42.2%)，
其中電子零組件增 25.3 億美元(+50.3%)、資通與視聽產品增 12.6
億美元(+41.1%)、礦產品增 4.6 億美元(+1.5 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 44.5 億美元，較上年同月增 11.5 億美元(+34.8%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 6.6 億美元(+57.0%)、電子零組件增 4.0 億美
元(+1.1 倍)，基本金屬及其製品減 1.4 億美元(-31.0%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.6 億美元，較上年同月增8.9 億美元(+32.0%)，其中
電子零組件增 8.9 億美元(+76.7%)、 化學品增0.5 億美元(+32.3%)，
資通與視聽產品減 0.6 億美元(-13.9%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月對美國出口 1,803.2 億美元，較上年同期增 631.6 億美
元(+53.9%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,394.7 億美元，增 307.5 億美
元(+28.3%)， 對東協出口1,150.7 億美元 ， 增372.3 億美元(+47.8%)，
對歐洲出口 376.6 億美元 ， 增130.7 億美元(+53.1%)， 對日本出口247.0
億美元，增 54.7 億美元(+28.4%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月自中國大陸與香港進口 126.8 億美元 ， 較上年同月增47.6 億美元
(+60.2%)，自東協進口 106.9 億美元，增46.7 億美元(+77.6%)，自南
韓進口 91.7 億美元，增29.6 億美元(+47.7%)，自日本進口 57.0 億美
元，增 11.2 億美元(+24.4%)，自美國進口 53.9 億美元，增 14.3 億美
元(+35.9%)，自歐洲進口 49.5 億美元，增 6.2 億美元(+14.3%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增75.4%、
自南韓增 63.2%、自中國大陸與香港增 40.5%、自美國增 24.4%、自
日本增 24.1%、自歐洲增 12.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-7 月主要國家/地區外貿景氣普遍上升，南韓(1-8 月)、香港、
新加坡出口動能尤為強勁，分別年增 52.8%、46.6%、38.3%；中國
大陸(1-8 月)、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-6 月)、日
本各年增 10.9%、8.1%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-7 月以香港進口年增 48.1%最
為顯著， 新加坡、 中國大陸(1-8 月)分別年增 37.0%、27.0%，南韓(1-8
月)、德國(1-6 月)各增 18.7%、11.7%，日本、美國各增 6.5%、1.2%。

圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）得益於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及 雲端服務等應用 需求強勁， 加
以外銷產品價格攀升，8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，年
增 41.0%，連續 34 個月正成長；進口 601.0 億美元，年增 44.3%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求擴張 ，及農工原料行情上
漲；出、進口互抵後，出超 223.0 億美元，亦創單月新高 。累計 1
至 8 月出口年增 44.2%、進口增 40.4%。
（二）8 月出口主要貨品以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品貢獻最多，年增率
分別為 58.0%及 41.8%，前者受惠 AI 商機及記憶體外銷價量齊揚，規
模值創單月新高，兩類併計增 49.3%，其餘貨類平均增 16.3%，以礦
產品隨油品售價上漲，年增 70.2%較為突出，基本金屬及其製品、電
機產品各增 22.4%、22.3%。累計 1 至 8 月資通與視聽產品出口值已
超越歷年水準，與電子零組件合計占總出口比重達 78.7%。

（三）8 月對主要市場出口同呈雙位數成長，其中對中國大陸與香港、日本
受電子零組件外銷強勁推升，出口值同創單月新高，各年增 39.3%、
32.0%，對東協、歐洲、美國分別增 42.2%、34.8%、20.7%；五大市
場之外，對墨西哥及澳大利亞出口皆增逾 2 倍。累計 1 至 8 月對美
國、歐洲出口各年增 53.9%、53.1%，對東協年增 47.8%，對日本、
陸港各增 28.4%、28.3%，對五大市場出口規模均為歷年同期最佳。
（四）展望未來，地緣政治衝突 及美國關稅政策變化等不確定 因素，持續
干擾全球經濟成長力道，惟隨人工智慧應用 不斷深化，算力需求攀
升，國際雲端服務業者加速建置 AI 基礎設施，加以國內半導體及伺
服器等供應鏈具競爭優勢，均有助支撐我國外銷動能，下半年出口
可望維持成長態勢。

What’s new?
AMD 以約  82 億美元全股票交易（ All-Stock Transaction ）收購由 AI 教⺟李
⾶⾶聯合創⽴的空間智能初創公司  World Labs ，是其繼收購賽靈思（ Xilinx,
500 億美元）、 Silo AI （ 6.65 億美元）及  ZT Systems （ 49 億美元）後，在
AI 領域另⼀前沿戰略布局。
依據協議，李⾶⾶將出任  AMD 執⾏副總裁兼⾸席科學家（ EVP & Chief
Scientist ），直接向董事⻑兼執⾏⻑蘇姿丰（ Lisa Su ）匯報，核⼼團隊（包括
共同創辦⼈  Justin Johnson 與  Ben Mildenhall ）將組成  AMD 旗下的前沿
AI 研究機構。這項交易象徵  AMD 從純硬體晶⽚商向「全端  AI 平台（ Full-
Stack AI Platform ）」的戰略轉移。

### Page 2

Synergies
1. Hardware-Software Co-Design ）
過去  AMD 在  AI 戰場的最⼤痛點是「先定義晶⽚硬體，再由軟體團隊艱難適
配  ROCm 」，導致⽣態進度⻑年落後  NVIDIA CUDA 。
模型導向架構（ Model-driven Silicon ）： World Labs 專注於空間智
能（ Spatial Intelligence ）與⼤型世界模型（ Large World Models,
LWMs ），涉及⾼並發的  3D 體積重建（ NeRF 、 3D Gaussian
Splatting ）、物理碰撞模擬及時空連續性計算。
付費訂閱戶可分享我們觀察到的 3 個併購綜效， 4 個⾵
險，尤其是股權稀釋及商譽攤銷對股東的影響，尤其是
未來的觀察指標
Subscribed
反哺硬體規格：頂級模型團隊直接進駐，能讓  AMD 在規劃下⼀代
Instinct 架構（如  MI400 系列及後續  UDNA 架構）時，精準針對底層
張量核⼼、記憶體⼦系統（ HBM 頻寬配置與容量）、晶⽚間互連

（ Inﬁnity Fabric ）進⾏客製化最佳化，擺脫被動追趕的窘境。

### Page 3

Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
2. Shifting focus to physical AI & embodied AI
當前以⽂字與  2D 圖像為核⼼的⽣成式  AI 正逐步成熟，下⼀階段的兵家必爭
之地是理解物理規律、三維環境模擬與機器⼈交互。
抗衡  NVIDIA Omniverse 與  Cosmos： NVIDIA 憑藉  Omniverse
數位孿⽣與  Cosmos 世界基礎模型，深度綁定⾃駕⾞、機器⼈與⼯業製
造業者。 AMD 過去在此領域近乎空⽩， World Labs 的加⼊補⿑了關鍵
資產，使  AMD 具備建構開放式物理世界模擬平台的基⽯。
3. Buying elite AI software engineers
⼈才磁吸效應：李⾶⾶在⼈⼯智慧與計算機視覺領域具備學術威望。由其
擔任⾸席科學家，能⼤幅扭轉  AMD 「⼯程實⼒強但缺乏  AI 頂級科學家
陣容」的市場認知，對後續吸納全球頂尖演算法研究員與跨界⼈才具備指

標性意義。
推動開源社群擴張： World Labs 團隊的⼊駐，能從演算法源頭優化
ROCm 開源軟體堆疊對前沿多模態架構的⽀援，降低開發者在  AMD 硬
體上訓練與微調複雜模型的阻⼒。
Risks and challenges
Source: Andrew Lu on global semis/techs
1. 財務溢價過⾼與商譽減損⾵險
World Labs 於  2024 年成⽴，早期融資估值約在  10 億美元左右，本次
AMD ⽀付  82 億美元，隱含極⾼的收購溢價。
儘管採全股票收購避免了現⾦儲備消耗，但會直接稀釋流通股東權益。
若未來  3 ⾄  5 年內該技術未能實質帶動  Instinct 晶⽚或系統出貨量激
增， AMD 將⾯臨商譽（ Goodwill ）與無形資產減損的提列壓⼒。我們⽬
前預測  AMD 未來 5 年每年將額外攤銷 15 億美元軟體資產溢價，讓年度折

### Page 4

舊及攤銷從 30 億美元增加 50% 到 45 億美元，除了 3-4% 股權稀釋外，影
響 0.9 美元年度 GAAP EPS 或 2027 年度彭博社分析師預期的  13.66 美元
GAAP EPS 的 6.6% ，但未來 5 年  Non-GAAP EPS 預測不受影響。
2. 研發燃燒率（ R&D Cash Burn ）與商業化空窗期
世界模型與物理模擬訓練需要極端龐⼤的算⼒資源與⼯程投⼊。空間智能⽬前
距離廣泛的企業級商業變現仍有⼀定距離，短期內  World Labs 不僅無法貢獻
⾃由現⾦流，還需佔⽤⼤量內部  GPU 叢集與研發預算，可能在短期內對
AMD 的⾮  GAAP 營運利潤率（ Operating Margin ）造成壓⼒。
3. 組織⽂化整合與頂尖⼈才留任
硬體半導體企業的⽂化核⼼是「嚴格的產品進度表、良率提升與供應鏈管
控」；⽽前沿  AI 實驗室的核⼼則是「開放式論⽂發表、⾃由度極⾼的算⼒
探索」。
過去⼤廠收購純研究型  AI 團隊後，常因官僚流程或研究⾃由受限導致核

⼼⼈才流失。李⾶⾶團隊能否在體量龐⼤的半導體公司架構內保持研究活
⼒，取決於管理層授予的獨⽴性與激勵機制設計。
4. 與下游客戶的競合衝突（ Channel Conﬂict ）
AMD 先前定位是純粹的中⽴算⼒供應商，⼒求承接微軟、 Meta 、 AWS
等不想被  NVIDIA 壟斷的轉單需求。
當  AMD ⾃⾝具備由李⾶⾶領軍的前沿模型團隊後，如何在「⾃研前沿模
型技術」與「⽀援客戶⾃研模型」之間維持中⽴界線，需謹慎拿捏，避免
讓雲端巨頭與下游  AI 新創產⽣戒⼼。
The points in future to check
後續評估此項併購成效的核⼼觀察點包含：
1. 硬體轉化速度： World Labs 的演算法研究能否在  12–18 個⽉內實質轉
化為  AMD UDNA/ROCm 上的專屬優化庫，並直接體現在  MI400 等下
⼀代晶⽚的基準測試（ Benchmarks ）上。
2. ⼈才留存率：李⾶⾶及其核⼼研究團隊在交易完成後的留任年限與論⽂ /
架構產出穩定度。

3. ⽣態聯盟建⽴： AMD 是否能以  World Labs 為核⼼，聯合⾃駕⾞、機器
⼈、遊戲引擎（如  Epic/Unity ）及⼯業製造巨頭，建⽴抗衡  NVIDIA
Omniverse 的開源標準體系。
這項交易本質上是  AMD 在完成晶⽚與系統（ ZT Systems ）拼圖後，向  AI
價值鏈最⾼端「認知架構與世界模型」邁出的防禦兼進攻型豪賭；若整合順
暢， AMD 將具備從底層矽智財到實體  AI 基礎模型的端到端定義能⼒，但其昂
貴的代價與漫⻑的技術轉化週期，將⾼度考驗管理層的整合與資本配置能⼒。

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Any forecast rests on assumptions. Each might be
individually reasonable, but if they all tilt in the same
direction, they look less plausible in combination.
• Different assumptions can also drive different conclusions,
as can be seen in Treasury and RBA views on trend growth

in supply capacity. Treasury’s long-term view on both
productivity growth and labour force participation trends is
systematically less downbeat than the RBA’s (shorter-term)
view. Treasury’s estimate of the stable-inflation rate of
unemployment is also lower than the RBA’s estimate.
• We consider the risks around the RBA’s view to be
asymmetric. The RBA’s assumptions implicitly require a
repeat of the factors that dragged recent productivity
outcomes down, despite strong global investment, policy

changes domestically and the possible benefits of AI. Some
observers appear to extrapolate this weakness beyond the
RBA’s forecast horizon. But this would be hard to square
with the reasonable belief that the global interest rate
structure will be higher in future than it was pre-pandemic.
A systematic tilt, even if unintentional and understandable,
may point to the likely direction of future surprises.
Any forecast rests on assumptions, and the further out

you forecast, the harder it is to ground those assumptions
in something real. Add the uncertainty about AI, where
predictions range from “unbounded prosperity without
needing to work” to “we will all be dead in a couple of years”,
and you can see why assuming the future looks like the past is
an attractive fall-back position.
How much of the past, though? This is the context for the
debate about the Intergenerational Review (IGR)  released this
week. In one corner, Treasury assumes that future productivity

growth will average 1.2%yr over the next 40 years, in line with
the 30-year average, and that labour force participation will
continue to trend up, as it has for the past five decades, until
2040. In the other corner, the RBA assumes that productivity
growth will average 0.7%yr, in line with the past 20 years
– essentially the post-GFC and pandemic period – and that
participation rates will be flat to down in the near term.
Both productivity assumptions have limitations: round-number-

of-year averages might be distorted by capturing a downturn
without the matching upturn, or vice versa. Peak-to-peak or
trough-to-trough is always preferable. Even that calculation
cannot escape the weakness of recent years, though.
When your assumptions have only upside risk
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
To be fair, the RBA’s productivity assumptions relate only to
the short term and are not strictly comparable to the IGR,
as the Governor has emphasised this week.  By contrast, the

near-term divergence in forecasts for the participation rate,
which my colleague Ryan Wells  first highlighted in May , is a
genuine divergence.
The RBA’s more downbeat set of assumptions could be
unwittingly contributing to a self-fulfilling talking-down
of Australia’s prospects that clearly appeals to some
commentators outside the public sector. Many in this group
view productivity growth as stemming wholly or mostly from
policy “reforms”, harking back to the halcyon Hawke–Keating

era of deregulation. Proponents point to the lack of reform
recently to justify this downbeat view of growth capacity. We
think this misses the global nature of that slowdown.
Part of the trouble with the downbeat view of future
productivity is that it implicitly assumes that the factors
weighing on productivity growth in the past will continue.
But there are good reasons not to expect the same post-GFC
weakness in risk appetite, and so investment and capital

accumulation, decades later and absent another crisis. The AI
boom and associated resurgence of risk appetite make it hard
to argue that we will see the same lack of new productivity-
enhancing technologies and general malaise as that period.
For Australia specifically, assuming the same low average
productivity growth as the past 20 years would require
assuming a compositional drag s imilar to a repeat of the
run-up of the care economy and decline in measured mining

production over the past 15 years. To be fair, decarbonisation
does imply further declines in (capital-intensive, high-
productivity) coal production, dragging mining productivity
down further. It would be a stretch, though, to assume
expansion in critical and transition minerals provides no offset.
We can grant that AI’s impact on productivity will take a while
to come through and still expect some boost in coming years
– at least by the end of the RBA’s forecast horizon in 2028.

Indeed, some observers already see an AI-induced boost to
service-sector activity in the recent upside surprise in UK GDP.
Some productivity pessimists might want to argue that the
“RBA assumptions collectively imply a
downbeat view of supply growth."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
rest of the world will benefit from the AI boom, but somehow
not Australia, or not until later. Such an extraordinary claim
requires a mechanism explaining that divergence, though.
The Productivity Commission estimated back in December
that AI could boost multifactor productivity by 2.3%pt over

the course of the next decade, or roughly a quarter-point per
year extra growth on average – a big difference in the scheme
of usual growth rates in output and productivity. Labour
productivity growth would be boosted a little more; the data
centre boom is expanding the capital stock. But an estimate
published in December is unlikely to adequately capture the
step change in capability of the main models, especially the
coding and agentic harnesses, that emerged over the summer

of 2025/26. Given what we understand to be limited adoption
of AI in the Australian public sector, policymakers might also
be systematically underestimating both the technology’s
capabilities and the breadth of uptake in the private sector.
As an illustration of how downbeat you need to be to
extrapolate the RBA’s near-term assumption further out,
suppose that all AI does is make about 10% of the economy
10% more productive over four years. This extra 1% of

economy-wide productivity is well below most estimates
of the impact. It would nonetheless add a quarter-point on
annual productivity growth for four years, bringing a downbeat
0.7% assumption up to almost 1%.
A deeper challenge is that, beyond the RBA’s near-term
horizon, low productivity growth is hard to square with a
higher future average for interest rates globally.
We note, for example, recent comments by RBA Deputy
Governor Hauser, pointing to 700 years of bond yield data

to suggest that the post-GFC period of low interest rates
was an anomaly, something we noted two years ago. The
global structure of interest rates emerges to balance global
saving and investment, and stronger investment for AI and
other purposes is clearly in evidence. We find it implausible
to assume that all that investment happens, pushing up
global yields, and none of it delivers higher productivity. Even
increased defence spending has historically had productivity

payoffs, as Ukraine’s drone manufacturing ecosystem has
exemplified recently.
It is understandable that when inflation is above target, a
central bank would rather err on the side of caution than
techno-optimism. They know that being downbeat, and
running policy a little too tight, means a faster return of
inflation to target, which is no bad thing for an inflation-
targeting central bank.
It matters, though, when those understandable assumptions

are systematically one-sided. If there are arguments that
productivity growth over the next few years will be even lower
than the 0.7%yr 20-year average, we are yet to see them. In
contrast, there are reasonable arguments for it to be a bit higher.
Similarly, the RBA’s participation rate assumptions are low
given near-term demographic trends , and out of step with
Treasury’s revised projections. Today’s labour market data
for August shows the participation rate rising again, to just

12½ basis points below the January 2025 all-time high. This
supports the idea that RBA’s view on participation trends
is too downbeat. The IGR’s NAIRU (stable-inflation rate of
unemployment) assumption is a quarter-point or so below
RBA estimates revealed in various FOI requests and the
Governor’s comments this week.
Together, the three RBA assumptions imply weaker growth
in supply capacity and thus more inflationary pressure for any
given level of demand. If every one of your assumptions seems

individually plausible, but all skew in the one direction, it
starts to look like a pattern. The thumb on the scale is unlikely
to be intentional, but it does suggest the likely direction of
future surprises in the medium term.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
4
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: the importance of supply
This week, market participants have focused on discussions
between the US and China on trade, security and technology
as President Xi visits Washington.
Preliminary discussions between US Treasury Secretary

Bessent and Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng ahead of
President Xi’s arrival in Washington were characterised by
both sides as productive, and Treasury Secretary Bessent
subsequently announced that discussions in Washington
would leave trade negotiations for upcoming meetings
between the two leaders in Shenzhen and Miami. To allow
room for this deferral, the current trade truce will be extended
until January 2027. President Trump subsequently outlined

that discussions during President Xi’s Washington visit would
instead focus on security, technology and AI.
A more constructive tone was also seen between the US and
Iran overnight, if press reports are accurate. According to
Bloomberg and Reuters, sources suggest the US and Iran are
again exploring a staged deal which would see Iran re-open
the Strait of Hormuz and the US lift its blockade of Iranian
ports. Negotiations are occurring on the sidelines of the UN

General Assembly, with Qatari officials mediating.
President Trump continues to show little interest in a deal
before November’s mid-term elections, but the global
economy is under increasing pressure as the price of Brent oil
hovers between USD100 and USD110 while refining spreads
add additional impetus to refined product prices. The mooted
consideration of a potential US diesel export ban this week
makes clear that the US is not immune from this pressure

despite having significant domestic energy supply and refining
capacity, relative to their own demand.
The consequences of the current energy supply shock and other
disruptions the US have experienced over the past year remain
front of mind for FOMC members. Those speaking this week such
as NY and Cleveland Fed Presidents Williams and Hammack
highlighted the risk to inflation expectations from a series of
supply shocks when the economy is fully employed, and the

likelihood of an additional hike(s) being required to rein in current
inflation momentum and risks. Williams noted that expectations
of another hike by year end are "reasonable" and that the FOMC
have "a lot of work to do". Debt market’s concern remains acute,
having now almost priced in four hikes over the coming year and
taken the US 10-year above 5.00%, currently 5.20%.
Australia’s only data release this week was the August
Labour Force Survey . Employment surprised to the upside,

rising 39.5k. But, once again, new labour demand was more
than offset by supply as the participation rate bounced
0.2ppts higher to 67.1%, just shy of January 2025’s 115-year
high of 67.2%. As a result, the unemployment rate increased
from 4.5% to 4.6% and was close to rounding up to 4.7%
(4.65% to two decimal places).
There may be some residual seasonality in August’s print
related to the new survey collection model. However, the
underlying trend is still consistent with a gradual easing in the

labour market, seeing the unemployment rate drift higher. We
expect this to continue through to the first half of 2027.
The strength in labour force participation also speaks to the
broader issue of Australia’s supply capacity. As discussed
by Chief Economist Luci Ellis , the RBA’s assumptions around
participation, productivity and the NAIRU collectively
skew towards a weak assessment of supply capacity. For
participation in particular, the RBA’s near-term assumptions

are downbeat compared to recent demographic trends and
also sit below Treasury’s longer-term projections as set out in
the 2026 IGR. As above, the August LFS emphasises that slack
could emerge more quickly than the RBA’s forecasts imply
without an outright fall in employment.
Ahead of next week’s RBA meeting and inflation data,
our latest state-level analysis  provides perspective on the
persistence of underlying price pressures, particularly the

importance of housing-related components. For a broader
assessment of the fiscal backdrop and the implications for the
states, our latest state fiscal update  explores how the housing
market correction and changes to NDIS funding arrangements
are pressuring state finances.
Before turning to this week’s events offshore, it is important
to highlight that last Friday the Bank of Japan raised its
policy rate by 25bp to 1.25%. Policymakers maintained a

constructive assessment of the economy, pointing to resilient
consumption, ongoing labour market tightness, and solid
investment spending. More importantly, the Bank expressed
growing confidence that inflation is increasingly being
sustained by domestic wage and demand dynamics rather
than imported cost shocks alone.
In our view, the emphasis placed on improving underlying
inflation dynamics suggests policymakers are becoming
increasingly comfortable that inflation is able to be sustained by

domestic demand and wage growth. Normalisation is likely to
remain gradual, but the Bank appears firmly on a path towards
further tightening, with rates likely to reach 1.5% by mid-2027.
Elliot Clarke, Head of International Economics
Ryan Wells, Economist
Illiana Jain, Economist

### Page 5

5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Along for the ride
There was plenty of action in the markets this week, as global
interest rates continued to move higher, and New Zealand
was taken along for the ride. In the US, strong economic data
and resurgent oil prices have led the market to believe that

the central bank will need to get on with the job and hike
its policy rate further in the months ahead. Longer-term US
interest rates have continued to lift sharply, driving both a rise
in domestic interest rates and a fall in the New Zealand dollar,
with the trade-weighted index already 4% below what the
RBNZ assumed in its September Monetary Policy Statement.
While the September MPS worked to dampen market
expectations about the pace of future rate hikes, offshore

developments have seen market pricing for the OCR bounce back
and exceed previous levels. Interest rate markets are currently
pricing around a 75% chance of a hike at the 28 October policy
review, with a high chance of another hike on top of this at the 9
December review. We’d question whether the RBNZ’s thinking
has really shifted to that degree in the last few weeks though.
Notably, the RBNZ provided some unscheduled comments on
the monetary policy outlook this week, in what was otherwise

a very light local news week. In connection with a visit to
Dunedin by Governor Breman and other RBNZ leaders, the
RBNZ put out a media release that included the following:
“If higher oil prices persist, they are expected to result in
somewhat higher near-term inflation than we assumed in the
September Statement… As always, we will assess incoming
data and global developments ahead of our next decision in
October and remain focused on the outlook for inflation over

the medium term.”
Both of these statements are fairly benign and factual in
themselves. What’s more notable is that the RBNZ felt the
need to make a public comment at all; the occasion didn’t
require one. (The Governor’s presentation at a business lunch
was originally billed as being based on material from the
September MPS, with no new insights.)
If the intention was to guide market expectations for the
October meeting lower, it wasn’t entirely successful. Indeed,

the market initially fixated on the first part of the statement –
higher near-term inflation – and pushed pricing for an October
hike even higher on the day. While this was unwound the
following day, market pricing for OCR hikes remains higher
than it was at the start of the week.
As it stands, that leaves the RBNZ with the prospect of
rattling the market if it follows through with the October
pause that it signalled in September MPS. However, it’s

possible that the local and offshore economic data, together
with geopolitical developments, could shift market pricing in
the meantime. Domestically, the NZIER’s quarterly survey of
business opinion (6 Oct) and the September quarter CPI (22
Oct) will be especially important in that regard.
Turning to the data, the only release this week was our
Employment Confidence Index, produced in conjunction with
McDermott Miller. Compared to our consumer confidence

survey, there was a relatively weak bounce in employment
confidence in the September quarter. The index rose by 3.2
points to 86.3, leaving it at the second-lowest level on record
for this survey going back to 2004.
Perceptions of current job opportunities were unchanged at a
net -60%. This is the component of the survey that seems to
have the most predictive power for near-term labour market
conditions and is broadly consistent with the unemployment

rate holding at its current 11-year high of 5.6%. Hiring has
remained soft to an unusual degree for this stage of the
economic cycle, and those who are out of the workforce have
been finding it increasingly difficult to get in.
It’s clear that there is still substantial slack in the labour
market and little upward pressure on wages, which would
push back somewhat against the market’s view that the RBNZ
will need to pick up the pace and deliver two more OCR hikes

before Christmas. The RBNZ doesn’t need to have employment
formally in its mandate for it to be attentive to the signals
coming from the labour market.
The data flow picks up again next week, with monthly releases
for employment, building activity, and business and consumer
confidence – see our previews on the following page. The
other release of note is the Pre-Election Fiscal and Economic
Update (PREFU) on Tuesday. This ‘book opening’ exercise
is designed to create a level playing field, allowing parties

outside the current Government and the public more generally
to judge to the state of the public finances in the leadup to the
General Election on 7 November. In the case of the opposition
parties, it will also allow them to calibrate the spending and
taxation policies on which they will campaign. See our preview
for the details on what we expect from this release.
Michael Gordon
Senior Economist

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Aug Household Spending Indicator (%mth)
Sep 29, Last: 1.1%, WBC f/c: –0.5%
Mkt f/c: 0.3%, Range: –0.5% to 0.7%
The household spending indicator rose 1.1%mth in July,
comfortably exceeding expectations and extending the strong
momentum evident through the middle of the year. Annual

growth accelerated to 7.0%yr, the fastest pace since early
2023.
Our Westpac-DataX Card Tracker points to softer spending
conditions through August and early September, with higher
fuel prices increasingly driving spending growth and, at the
margin, squeezing spending elsewhere. The detail suggests
a modest rotation away from discretionary categories, with
hospitality spending easing and vehicle sales also likely
moderating after a strong June quarter. Together with a softer

read from our MHSI-aligned card measure, we anticipate the
household spending indicator will decline 0.5%mth in August.
AUS: RBA Policy Decision (%)
Sep 29, Last: 4.35, Westpac f/c: 4.60
Market f/c: 4.60, Range: 4.60
Westpac anticipates the RBA Monetary Policy Board will raise
the cash rate by 25bps to 4.60% at its September meeting.
As discussed by Chief Economist Luci Ellis recent rhetoric from
the RBA have escalated, particularly with Governor Bullock

flagging that upside inflation risks appear to be "materialising"
– the conditionality set in August for further rate hikes.
There is a risk of a follow-up hike, though it crucially depends
on the data flow after the meeting. For September, we
continue to expect a split vote at the meeting, with material
differences in opinion about trend growth in supply capacity,
as discussed on Page 2.
AUS: Aug Headline CPI (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: 1.0, Westpac f/c: 0.4

Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.4 to 0.9
The July CPI reversed May and June weakness with a larger
than expected 1.0% increase. This was stronger than
Westpac’s expectation of 0.8% and market’s expectation of
0.9%. The annual pace eased to 3.5%yr from 3.8%yr.
We are looking for a 0.4% increase in the August CPI. Food &
non-alcoholic beverages to rise 0.2%mth with solid gains for
bread & cereal products (0.4%), meat & seafood (0.5%) and
food products n.e.c. (0.4%) partially offset by falling non-

alcoholic beverages (–0.6%) and a soft print for meals out &
take away food (0.1%).
At two decimal points it is a 0.43% increase in August so the
risk this month is to the upside.
For more details please see “ August CPI Preview ”.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Aug Trimmed Mean CPI (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: 0.5, Westpac f/c: 0.2
Market f/c: 0.3, Range: 0.2 to 0.4
The Trimmed Mean (TM) lifted 0.5%mth/3.5%yr, on par with
last July which was the strongest monthly read in the series’
short history. The result was stronger than both our and the

market’s expectation of 0.4%mth.
August TM is expected to rise 0.2%mth lifting the annual pace
to 3.6%yr. For market services excluding volatiles and holiday
travel we have pencilled in a step down to 0.1%mth, this series
lifted 0.8% in July possibly due to the larger-than-expected
increase in minimum wages.
The distribution of the August TM is from a low of –0.32%mth
to a high of 0.53%mth. Remembering the TM is seasonally
adjusted, with rents (+0.17%mth) and dwellings (+0.20%mth)

mostly locking in the TM. See “ August CPI Preview ”.
AUS: Aug Dwelling Approvals (%mth)
Sep 30, Last: –3.6, Westpac f/c: –5.0
Market f/c: –1.0, Range: –6.0 to 1.0
Dwelling approvals fell 3.6%mth in July, with annual growth
easing to 9.0%yr. The decline was driven primarily by detached
dwellings, although unit approvals also edged lower. The
result suggests higher interest rates may be starting to weigh
on new housing demand.
We expect a similar story to play out in August, with

elevated interest rates continuing to weigh on detached
dwelling approvals, while the unit segment holds broadly
steady. Overall, dwelling approvals are expected to decline
5%mth. While risks remain tilted to the downside, residential
construction should remain relatively resilient through 2026,
supported by the strong lift in approvals through the first half
of the year and a sizeable pipeline of work yet to be done.
AUS: Aug Private Sector Credit (%mth)

Sep 30, Last: 0.6, Westpac f/c: 0.5
Market f/c: 0.5, Range: 0.3 to 0.6
Growth in private sector credit growth appeared unsustainable
over May-Jun, especially given the slowing in the housing
market. Come July, the pace now dropped from 0.8%mth to
0.6%mth. Housing credit the main source of weakness, with
investor credit growth beginning to adjust lower in response to
higher interest rates, lower house prices and, most importantly,
tax changes for investment properties. Meanwhile, business

borrowing continued to rise rapidly.
For August, we have pencilled in a 0.5%mth increase, and we
look for continued divergence between housing and business
segments.

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.8
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
AUS: Sep Cotality Home Value Index (%mth)
Oct 1, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: –0.9
The Cotality home value index – covering all of Australia – fell
0.9% in August following downwardly-revised 1.2% and 0.9%
declines in July and June. Annual growth slowed to 2.7%yr.

Prices nationally are now 3.6% below their peak in March.
The September update is set to see more of the same with
the daily measure (covering the five major capital cities rather
than all of Australia) tracking a 1.2% decline in line with
this measure’s August move. Annual growth looks like to
stall about flat. The detail suggests monthly declines have
accelerated in Brisbane, Perth and Adelaide but moderated a
touch in Melbourne, noting that there is a seasonal element

to price moves in Sep. Markets are still adjusting to higher
interest rates and the tax policy changes introduced in the May
Federal budget with more weakness likely into year-end.
AUS: Aug Goods Trade Balance ($bn)
Oct 1, Last: 1.9, Westpac f/c: 2.3
Market f/c: 2.2, Range: 1.5 to 2.5
The goods trade surplus narrowed slightly to $1.9bn in July
but remained above our and consensus expectations. This
was down from an upwardly revised surplus of $2.3bn in June.

Underneath the modest headline change was considerable
volatility among components, especially in flows of non-
monetary gold, remain the chief culprit.
For August, we have pencilled in a slight widening of the trade
surplus to $2.3bn. Port data points to a bounce-back in coal
and iron ore export volumes,  and prices looked to have edged
higher. However, volatility in non-monetary gold trade and
other hard-to-predict sub-categories (like ADP equipment)

remain a wildcard.
NZ: Aug Monthly Employment Indicator (%mth)
Sep 28, Last: 0.3, Westpac f/c: 0.3
The MEI has continued to tick higher in recent months,
showing that the jobs market has held its ground through
the Iran conflict and the consequent surge in fuel prices. The
weekly snapshots provided by Stats NZ have been running
ahead of year-ago levels and suggest another solid result
for August – we’ve pencilled in a 0.3% increase, following
a similar rise in July. However, the history of this indicator

suggests that both months are likely to be revised down a bit
from their initial release.

### Page 9

Nonfarm payrolls surprised to the upside in August, 
registering a gain of 162k with 55k in back revisions to June 
and July. The report left the 3-month average at 71k, near the 
top of the estimated range consitent with balance between 
labour demand and supply. Come September, the trend is 
expected to remain in place with a 80k gain forecast. With 
inflation risks paramount, upward revisions will have a greater 
impact on market variables than a negative revision.
The decline in the participation rate over the past year has 
capped the unemployment rate. Participation is likely to edge 
higher in September and take the unemployment rate up 
to 4.2% with it. There is little available slack in the labour 
market, but plenty just outside if demand lifts.

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng không Taseco (AST) 
Tiếp tục mở rộng điểm kinh doanh 
22/09/2026  
Chuyên viên cao cấp Nguyễn Ngọc Anh 
(+84) 24-7303-5333 anhntn@kbsec.com.vn

KQKD tăng trưởng tích cực trong 
2Q2026

Tăng trưởng khách qua cảng hàng 
không thúc đẩy doanh thu tại các 
điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu

Mở rộng mạng lưới tạo động lực tăng 
trưởng doanh thu mới

Cơ cấu kinh doanh và hiệu quả vận 
hành hỗ trợ cải thiện biên lợi nhuận

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA, giá mục 
tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu

2Q2026, AST ghi nhận doanh thu và LNST đạt 479/92 tỷ VND (+22%/19% yoy), với 
động lực chủ yếu đến từ (1) đóng góp từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn 
Nhất và (2) tăng trưởng tích cực của các mảng hàng miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood 
và phòng chờ thương gia. Lũy kế 6T2026, doanh thu và LNST đạt 1,035/221 tỷ VND 
(+33%/46% yoy), lần lượt hoàn thành 48%/49% kế hoạch năm.

Chúng tôi dự phóng lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa tại các thị trường AST khai thác 
tăng bình quân 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. Lưu lượng và tỷ trọng khách quốc tế 
gia tăng được kỳ vọng thúc đẩy số giao dịch và doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi 
khách, đặc biệt tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Chúng tôi dự phóng số điểm kinh doanh AST tăng từ 145 điểm cuối năm 2026 lên 
182 điểm năm 2030. Sau đóng góp từ 19 điểm tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất và 6 điểm mới tại 
T2 Nội Bài từ 2025 - 2026, các dự án Long Thành, Phú Quốc và Gia Bình tiếp tục mở 
ra dư địa mở rộng mạng lưới trong trung và dài hạn cho doanh nghiệp.

Việc mở rộng các hoạt động mang lại lợi nhuận cao, bao gồm hàng miễn thuế, 
phòng chờ thương gia và tối ưu hoạt động tại các điểm kinh doanh được kỳ vọng 
tác động tích cực lên biên lợi nhuận của AST. Chúng tôi dự phóng biên lợi nhuận 
gộp đạt 64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027, hỗ trợ LNST công ty mẹ tăng lần lượt 
48%/17% yoy.

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với AST, giá mục tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu, tương 
đương tiềm năng tăng giá 20% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 21/09/2026.

Cơ cấu doanh thu 2025 Hoạt động kinh doanh

CTCP Dịch vụ Hàng không Taseco là một 
trong những doanh nghiệp có quy mô mạng 
lưới điểm kinh doanh lớn trong lĩnh vực dịch 
vụ phi hàng không tại Việt Nam, với 141 
điểm kinh doanh tại 6 sân bay quốc tế tính 
đến cuối tháng 6/2026. Hoạt động cốt lõi của 
công ty bao gồm bán lẻ bách hóa lưu niệm 
(BHLN), hàng miễn thuế, dịch vụ ăn uống và 
phòng chờ thương gia. Ngoài ra, AST còn 
tham gia lĩnh vực suất ăn hàng không thông 
qua khoản đầu tư tại công ty liên kết VinaCS. 
Nguồn: Báo cáo công ty, KBSV

Điểm nhấn đầu tư  Chú thích 
Doanh thu trên các điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu tăng nhờ lượng khách quốc tế/nội địa dự phóng tăng 
11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Mở rộng mạng lưới tạo động lực tăng trưởng mới, với số điểm kinh doanh AST 2026 dự kiến đạt 145 
điểm và tăng lên 182 điểm vào 2030. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo 
Biên lợi nhuận gộp cải thiện lên 64.7%/65.5% trong năm 2026/2027 nhờ mở rộng hàng miễn thuế, 
phòng chờ và tối ưu vận hành. 
 Chi tiết ở phần dưới của báo cáo

Biểu đồ 5. Doanh thu/điểm kinh doanh của AST

Nguồn:AST

Tăng trưởng khách qua cảng hàng 
không thúc đẩy doanh thu tại các 
điểm kinh doanh hiện hữu 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng tăng trưởng lưu lượng hành khách, đặc biệt là sự gia tăng tỷ 
trọng khách quốc tế, sẽ thúc đẩy doanh thu và hiệu quả kinh doanh của AST 
trong giai đoạn 2026–2030. Theo Cục Hàng không Việt Nam, lượng khách thông 
qua các cảng hàng không trong 7T2026 đạt hơn 76 triệu lượt (+7% yoy), trong 
đó khách quốc tế đạt gần 30 triệu lượt (+11% yoy), trong khi khách nội địa ghi 
nhận tăng trưởng 4% yoy. Xu hướng này được hỗ trợ bởi nhu cầu du lịch quốc 
tế, chính sách thị thực thuận lợi và việc mở rộng mạng bay, trong khi tăng 
trưởng kinh tế và thu nhập tiếp tục tạo nền tảng cho nhu cầu đi lại trong nước. 
Trên cơ sở đó, chúng tôi dự phóng lượt khách quốc tế và nội địa tại các thị 
trường AST khai thác tăng bình quân lần lượt 11% và 5%/năm đến 2030, qua đó 
nâng dần tỷ trọng khách quốc tế trong tổng lưu lượng, thúc đẩy doanh 
thu/điểm kinh doanh nhờ: (1) lưu lượng hành khách qua cảng hàng không gia 
tăng và (2) tỷ trọng khách quốc tế cao hơn hỗ trợ doanh thu bình quân trên mỗi 
khách, đặc biệt tại mảng miễn thuế, bán lẻ và phòng chờ.

Biểu đồ 6. Lượt khách qua cảng hàng không Việt Nam (triệu 
lượt) 
 Biểu đồ 7. Doanh thu/điểm kinh doanh AST (tỷ VND)

Nguồn: AST  Nguồn: AST

Mở rộng mạng lưới tại các nhà ga 
hiện hữu và sân bay mới, gia tăng 
quy mô kinh doanh đến 2030 
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng chu kỳ mở rộng hạ tầng hàng không giai đoạn 2026–2030 sẽ 
tạo dư địa gia tăng quy mô kinh doanh cho AST, với số điểm kinh doanh dự 
phóng đạt 145 điểm vào cuối 2026 và tăng lên 182 điểm vào năm 2030. Cụ thể: 
- Trong ngắn hạn, tăng trưởng năm 2026 được hỗ trợ bởi việc ghi nhận 
đủ năm doanh thu từ 19 điểm kinh doanh mở mới tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất 
trong năm 2025, cùng 6 điểm mới tại T2 Nội Bài trong năm 2026, trong 
đó có 2 cửa hàng miễn thuế và 2 cửa hàng Memories of Vietnam khai 
trương trong 2Q2026. 
- Trong trung và dài hạn, các dự án Cảng HKQT Long Thành, Cảng HKQT 
Phú Quốc nâng cấp mở rộng và Cảng HKQT Gia Bình sẽ góp phần thúc 
đẩy ngành hàng không Việt Nam nói chung, bổ sung mặt bằng thương 
mại, mở ra cơ hội phát triển các mảng kinh doanh cốt lõi cho AST nói 
riêng bao gồm các mảng bán lẻ, miễn thuế, nhà hàng - fastfood và 
phòng chờ thương gia. Với kinh nghiệm khai thác 141 điểm kinh doanh 
tại 6 sân bay quốc tế và lịch sử trúng thầu tại T3 Tân Sơn Nhất, T2 Nội 
Bài trong thời gian qua, chúng tôi kỳ vọng AST có khả năng cạnh tranh 
cao trong việc đấu thầu quyền khai thác mặt bằng tại các sân bay mới, 
đặc biệt là Gia Bình, qua đó duy trì vị thế và thị phần kinh doanh phi 
hàng không tại khu vực phía Bắc khi một phần lưu lượng hành khách 
được phân bổ lại từ Nội Bài.

Cơ cấu kinh doanh và hiệu quả vận 
hành hỗ trợ mở rộng biên lợi nhuận  
 Chúng tôi kỳ vọng biên lợi nhuận của AST tiếp tục cải thiện trong 2026 - 2027, 
nhờ định hướng mở rộng các mảng có biên lợi nhuận tốt như hàng miễn thuế 
và phòng chờ thương gia, kết hợp tối ưu hiệu quả vận hành tại các điểm kinh 
doanh. Cụ thể: (1) AST ưu tiên gia tăng số điểm kinh doanh hàng miễn thuế 
nhằm đón đầu tăng trưởng nhanh và ổn định của lượng khách quốc tế, qua đó 
nâng đóng góp lợi nhuận từ hoạt động mua sắm tại sân bay, (2) mở rộng mạng 
lưới phòng chờ, đồng thời gia tăng lượng khách sử dụng trên nền năng lực phục 
vụ hiện hữu và (3) tối ưu hóa hiệu quả hoạt động thông qua cải thiện bố trí mặt 
bằng, danh mục sản phẩm và kiểm soát chi phí tại các điểm kinh doanh, qua 
đó tăng doanh thu và lợi nhuận thu được trên mỗi khách hàng.

Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA, giá 
mục tiêu 76,500 VND/cổ phiếu 
 Chúng tôi khuyến nghị MUA đối với cổ phiếu AST, với giá mục tiêu 76,500 
VND/cổ phiếu, tương đương tiềm năng tăng giá 20% so với giá đóng cửa ngày 
21/09/2026, dựa trên triển vọng tăng trưởng từ lượng khách quốc tế, mở rộng 
mạng lưới điểm kinh doanh và khả năng cải thiện biên lợi nhuận của doanh 
nghiệp. Chúng tôi sử dụng phương pháp định giá từng phần (SOTP), bao gồm 
DCF cho hoạt động kinh doanh cốt lõi của AST và P/E mục tiêu 11x đối với phần 
lợi nhuận thuộc sở hữu AST tại VinaCS. Các giả định chính gồm: (1) lượng khách 
quốc tế/nội địa tăng trung bình 11%/5% mỗi năm đến 2030, (2) số điểm kinh 
doanh tăng từ 145 điểm cuối 2026 lên 182 điểm năm 2030, với giả định AST 
trúng thầu mặt bằng tại cả Long Thành và Gia Bình và (3) biên lợi nhuận gộp 
2026/2027 đạt 64.7/65.5%. Đối với VinaCS, chúng tôi kỳ vọng nhà máy suất ăn 
Long Thành sẽ hỗ trợ tăng trưởng lợi nhuận trong trung và dài hạn khi dự kiến 
có thể bắt đầu đóng góp lợi nhuận cho AST từ 2028.

Rủi ro đối với định giá bao gồm: (1) AST không giành được quyền khai thác tại 
Long Thành và Gia Bình như kỳ vọng, (2) lưu lượng khách dịch chuyển giữa các 
sân bay mới và hiện hữu khiến doanh thu tại một số điểm kinh doanh bị sụt 
giảm, (3) cạnh tranh gia tăng từ các doanh nghiệp kinh doanh dịch vụ phi hàng 
không khác và (4) căng thẳng địa chính trị kéo dài, giá dầu tăng gây áp lực lên 
hoạt động hàng không và chi tiêu của hành khách.

Document 228: Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital

Source PDF: all report pdf/Global Strategy Views_ Competition for Capital.pdf
Report source: Goldman Sachs Publishing
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 12
Status: ok

### Page 1

1. There are two themes dominating our investor conversations: the impact of AI and 
the rise in interest rates. The two issues are linked as demand for capital from both 
the private and public sectors increase. In the private sector a surge in capex 
spending to fund AI infrastructure has eaten into free cash flow and forced 
companies to raise more in debt and equity markets. Meanwhile, government 
borrowing needs have increased as priorities shift towards upgrading critical 
infrastructure, energy security and defense at a time when cyclical inflationary 
pressures driven by higher energy prices are also resulting in higher policy rates. The 
combination has pushed up the cost of capital. As recently as 2022, for example, 
30-year bond yields in Germany and Japan were close to zero (Exhibit 1). A rise in 
yields, together with more uncertainty (over geopolitics and the future impact of AI) 
have, in combination, pushed up the cost of capital.

2. This rise in yields comes after a near-record period of equity outperformance 
relative to bonds over 10-year holding periods (Exhibit 2).

Exhibit 1: Rising yields and uncertainty have increased the cost of capital 
30-year government bond benchmark yields (%) 
          













### Page 2

As equities have continued to outperform bonds, equity risk premia have fallen back to 
levels last seen in the late 1990s, leaving equity markets more vulnerable to further 
increases in bond yields. That said, in the US and Japan, the ERP has bounced off recent 
lows (Exhibit 3).

3. The impact of rising bond yields, however, depends not only on the level of yields, but 
also on the reasons as well as the speed of the adjustment (Exhibit 4). It is the speed 
issue that has caused the biggest impact in the recent past. As our US strategists note, 
stocks have usually generated positive returns alongside rising interest rates unless the 
pace of rising rates exceeded 2+ standard deviations and this is a pattern that is broadly

the start of the year to 19x, in line with its long-run average, despite the market being

5. While US equities have driven the bulk of equity outperformance for much of the 
fifteen years post the financial crisis, the improvement in earnings across regions, and 
the sharper de-rating in the US, has resulted in equity performance being 
broader-based by geography. Since 2025, the US equity market has been the weakest of 
the major regions (Exhibit 6), a turnaround from the dominant trend since the financial 
crisis and increasing investor returns from a more geographically diversified portfolio.

6. Supportive profit growth has been accompanied by positive earnings revisions, with 
2026 and 2027 estimates moving higher across major regions. There are four broad 
areas which have been driving much of this profit growth. First, technology earnings 
continue to be very robust. Second, rising energy prices have pushed up profits in the 
commodity sector. Third, banks have generally enjoyed strong earnings backed by 
positive nominal GDP growth, steep yield curves and strong private sector balance 
sheets. Finally, sectors such as Industrials have benefited from the surge in AI capex 
spending which has spilled over into improved revenues for the ‘pick and shovels’ of AI 
infrastructure. The breadth of the earnings’ growth has also increased the opportunity 
for investors to diversify across sectors as well as countries.

7. While technology profit growth has remained strong, the surge in capex spending 
among the hyperscalers has increasingly eaten through their free cash flow (Exhibit 8) 
prompting companies to look for alternative sources of funding, in the credit and equity 
markets.  The capex spending growth for AA-rated issuers has been substantial: the 65% 
year-over-year growth in Q2 marks the 10th consecutive quarter that aggregate AA 
capex growth exceeds 35%. They have also turned to the convertible bond market 
where volume has also increased year to date reaching $135 billion in the US, with 
AI-related borrowers driving 44% of total issuance, on our estimates. 
Our Credit team raised their full-year US IG gross issuance forecast by $200bn to $2.3 
trillion and AI-related issuers account for a quarter of USD IG gross supply this year, 
pushing overall supply to a year-to-date record.

surge. Globally technology has seen its PE ratio moderate, falling below its 20-year 
median (Exhibit 10). Meanwhile, the spending of these companies has spilled over into 
better earnings and more visible cash flows in many other parts of the economy. 
Healthcare, Consumer Discretionary and Industrials are all more expensive in absolute 
terms than IT, and Industrials has re-rated and now has a PE close to the 90th percentile 
of its 20-year range.

9. For these reasons, we have argued that Technology equities have not experienced a 
classic bubble because valuation levels are much lower than in previous financial 
bubbles. Nevertheless, the risk for investors is less about valuation and more whether 
there is an unsustainable earnings bubble. Bubbles in earnings, rather than valuations, 
have occurred in previous periods.  The Bank sector, for example, briefly became the 
biggest sector in the S&P 500 in the run up to the financial crisis of 2008/09 (Exhibit 11). 
Unlike the technology bubble of the late 1990s, or the Japanese bubble of the late 
1980s, Bank stocks did not experience a major valuation bubble at the time, but the 
surge in earnings that drove the sector’s outperformance proved to be unsustainable.

### Page 8

There are, however, important differences to the Tech sector today. The earnings of the 
banks were boosted by sharply rising leverage and the asset that they were financing – 
US real estate – did experience a valuation bubble. When the housing market collapsed, 
pushing economies into recession, it triggered a sharp fall in underlying earnings for the 
banking sector. There are important differences today. First, technology profits remain 
very robust, and balance sheets are strong overall. Second, interest coverage ratios for 
the aggregate S&P 500 and its median stock, for example, rank in the 99th and 68th 
percentiles relative to the past 20 years. Third, demand for compute is accelerating and 
outstripping supply; Microsoft recently declared their intent to triple data centre 
capacity in 6 years. The bottlenecks are increasingly power, land and data centre 
capacity rather than a lack of compute demand. At our annual Communacopia 
Technology Conference, Nvidia reiterated its 2030 AI TAM outlook to $3tn-$4tn and 
supply constraints continue to be broad based. Nonetheless, any slowdown in profit 
growth, in an environment of a much higher cost of capital, could put downward 
pressure on equity prices, reducing confidence in future cash flows across the ecosystem 
from the hyperscalers to the ‘pick and shovels’ that have been benefiting from the capex 
boom.  
10. Given the near-term risks in bond markets, we remain neutral on equities, alongside 
other asset classes on a 3-month horizon, but continue to be overweight equities over 
12 months. While profit growth is likely to slow from the recent strong pace, ongoing 
growth in nominal GDP should underpin equity markets and the earnings cycle 
propelling equity returns even without any valuation expansion from current levels and 
our economists continue to see a low risk of recession. As our asset allocation team has 
shown, earnings growth and ROE are likely to continue to be the key drivers of equity 
performance relative to bonds, and we expect these fundamentals to remain supportive 
for equity returns. Nevertheless, we continue to recommend well diversified equity 
exposure as opportunities continue to broaden across geographies, sectors and factors.

Stock correlations are at multi-year lows across the major equity markets (Exhibit 12).

has also seen significant dispersion of returns. Differentiation, diversification and alpha 
generation are likely to remain the key drivers of equity returns. With higher bond yields, 
valuations are not likely to rise making earnings the critical driver of returns for investors.

Miha Hribernik | Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties |  September 2026
The Trump-Xi meeting in Washington met our key expectation: It delivered a subdued-yet-constructive summit resulting in a modest number of concrete deliverables that will minimise the near-term risks of abrupt
decoupling, a security crisis, or a disruptive trade war. This was the best plausible outcome for markets, given the structural nature of China-US competition. The brief two-month extension of the Busan agreement until

10 Jan 2027 will further delay new tariffs and export controls on both sides. It will also increase pressure to deliver a broader trade and investment package, and further trade-truce extensions, by the end of the year. We
anticipate more talks between US Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent and Chinese Vice Premier He Lifeng in Q4 as both sides seek a larger deal.
For China, stability with Washington will likely remain a priority as it manages rising trade tensions with the EU and prepares for top leadership reshuffles at its 2027 Party Congress. Combined with shifting US public

perceptions of China, these factors could create near-term space for more dealmaking on the sidelines of the upcoming APEC (18-19 Nov) and G20 (14-15 Dec) summits. As our surveys below show, US voters are now
more open to increased trade and investment with China, even at the cost of geopolitical concessions, while concerns over the cost of living have intensified. Both trends are now at their highest levels since April.
Nevertheless, political guardrails are not a panacea for a relationship that remains characterized by mistrust and competing national security priorities. As the next slide will show, the outlook for China-US relations

becomes more complex beyond this near-term horizon….

### Page 3

Miha Hribernik | Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties |  September 2026
An analysis of the geopolitical calendar reveals a complex slate of tariffs, import curbs, export controls, and sensitive pol itical events. Even as leader-level diplomacy has reduced the risk of a near-term breakdown, it
has done little to stem the tide of new bilateral economic restrictions. These have proliferated during 2026 to include new e xport controls, sanctions, and tariffs (see list), with significant implications for high-growth

industries including drones, AI, electric vehicles, and robotics.
Competition is unlikely to slow, and events could repeatedly test the fragile trade truce. Q4 2026 will see further China-US dialogue and China-EU trade talks. Should talks with Brussels fall short of averting increased
European trade pressure, Beijing may further prioritise near-term US stability. But the coming months will also bring challenges in the form of new US overcapacity tariffs and polysilicon levies, while next year will usher

in curbs on critical minerals and additional semiconductor tariffs. The run-up to the 10 Jan Busan truce expiry could also bring new economic restrictions as both sides seek negotiating leverage. Both countries will also
hold major political events (China’s Party Congress in 2027 and US elections in 2028), with potential implications for bilateral ties, while Taiwan’s Jan 2028 election will be another key watchpoint.
But these are just the events whose outcomes and impacts we can estimate in advance. Understanding probable China-US developments that are not on the calendar is equally important for investors. We should

assume that competitive dynamics across myriad policy issues will drive other developments and policy changes, often announce d with little or no advance notice. We examine some of the key issues in the next slide.
The policy horizon: Key events to watch (2026–2029)
The next several years will see more summits, tariffs, major political events, and key elections

Miha Hribernik | Mapping the Next Phase of China-US Ties |  September 2026
Unless consistently managed, structural competition could move markets and test stability through the end of the
decade and beyond. We highlight five watchpoints for markets:
1. The AI race: Dialogue is set to strengthen AI safety guardrails, but national and economic security imperatives
mean it is unlikely to significantly slow the China-US AI race, much less meaningfully reverse existing restrictions.

Any attempts to pace AI development would touch on questions of tech restrictions, including Beijing’s perceptions
of US technological containment, which could increase amid potential restrictions on remote access to AI chips and
Chinese data centre components.
2. Tariff pressures: Trade tensions have subsided given a lower US trade deficit and the new Board of Trade, which is
set to reduce tariffs on US$60 billion of non-sensitive goods. But significant differences persist: Washington plans

to unveil new polysilicon and semiconductor tariffs, as well as overcapacity levies on 16 economies (including
China). The US is also seeking to restrict Chinese inputs in North American supply chains and target alleged
transshipment, while Beijing shores up its supply chain dominance in areas of US import dependence, such as
advanced drone components and processed critical minerals – which continue to be a source of friction.
3. Regulatory toolkits: The wider tech sector remains central to expanding export controls and restrictions. China is

strengthening its toolkit. Its 2026 codification of anti-derisking regulations strengthens Beijing’s ability to take
action over foreign sanctions or export controls. Meanwhile, its outbound investment regulations, effective 1 Jul,
cover technology, data, talent and capital. In the US, Congress is drafting legislation to change outbound
investment reporting requirements for biotech and biopharma, restrict government procurement of humanoid
robots and solar equipment, and ban the use of China-linked connected vehicles on military installations.

4. Geopolitical stress-tests: The war in Iran is unlikely to derail relations in the near term, but the conflict has strained
bilateral ties. Any potential broadening of the conflict in scale and intensity, or a further expansion of US sanctions
that impact Chinese interests, could test guardrails. Additionally, while the probability of conflict in the Taiwan
Strait remains low, differences over Taiwan could remain unresolved across successive administrations in both

Washington and Beijing into the 2030s and beyond.
5. Political anchors: Two domestic events will shape relations into the 2030s. Beijing’s priority is stability ahead of the
late 2027 Party Congress. For China, dialogue can help reduce the risk of costly escalation, potentially slow the
pace of new US economic restrictions, and buy time for efforts to strengthen self-reliance in strategic sectors such
as semiconductors (where China is aiming for 80% self-sufficiency by 2030).

In the US, a durable bipartisan consensus on China could make visible concessions to Beijing increasingly difficult
ahead of the 2028 presidential election. Recent bipartisan criticism of the Trump administration’s decision to allow
limited Nvidia H200 chip sales to China reflects the political constraints facing a transactional approach to China,
raising the prospect that guardrails will strain closer to election day.
Structural strains: Testing the guardrails

Navigating the trade, technology, and regional pressure points

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce, U.S. Department of Labor, IHS Markit, Federal Reserve Board and Wells Fargo Economics
Sector Analysis
Personal Consumption Expenditures (PCE)
• Consumer spending is holding up, and we expect real PCE to rise at around a 3.0% annualized rate
in Q3.
Consumer spending has been mixed, yet resilient in recent months. After a soft July, driven largely by

weaker durable goods purchases, retail sales rebounded strongly in August, rising at a 1.2% monthly
clip amid broadly distributed gains across retailers. The latest data have led us to lift our near-term
growth forecast, and we now look for real PCE to rise at a 3.2% annualized clip in Q3. Previous data
will also likely be revised higher on firmer control group sales data and strong services revenue in Q2,
suggesting recent consumer momentum remains intact. All told, the consumer is still in decent shape

and has capacity to keep spending. Real income growth remains modest but is beginning to improve
as inflation eases and the labor market remains broadly supportive suggesting continued consumption
even if that pace of spending may moderate into year-end.
Investment: Equipment, Intellectual Property Products and Inventories
• The AI build-out is still king in terms of the contribution to business investment, but the
broadening in capex spending beyond the tech sector is taking root.

AI-related investment continues to dominate capital allocation. There are no signs of firms scaling
back their outlays now, though at some point as AI investment matures, merely sustaining spending
at elevated levels will provide a smaller incremental boost to growth than it has in recent quarters.
Beyond AI, the tentative signs we've identified that signal a broadening in the investment backdrop
are getting a bit louder. The ISM manufacturing index has been in expansion territory for eight-

Economics | 3

### Page 4

Monthly Economics
straight months, and durable goods orders have firmed across a wider range of industries. Part of
this improvement likely reflects delayed capital spending plans finally moving forward, but inventory
rebuilding after five consecutive quarters of drawdown may be lending some support as well.

Source: U.S. Department of Commerce and Wells Fargo Economics
Investment: Residential
• Residential construction looks likely to taper through the rest of the year while mortgage rates and
new home inventories remain elevated.
Headwinds have intensified against residential construction. The trend in single-family construction is
downshifting, emblematic of the affordability challenges depressing buyer demand. As of this writing,

mortgage rates have risen to over 7.0% and builder confidence has declined alongside. Sales stuck in
a low gear mean that new home listings are inching higher, further discouraging new development.
Multifamily construction appears to be holding up but faces limited potential upside with vacancy
rates elevated across much of the South. We still see a potential for marginal improvement in 2027,
but higher mortgage rates dampen our outlook.
Investment: Nonresidential Structures

• Construction appears to be gaining steam, led by data centers and power. However, sluggish
commercial starts elsewhere and high interest rates are likely to keep growth relatively modest.
The AI infrastructure build out is supporting modest growth in nonresidential construction. After
moderating for a full year, private nonresidential outlays rose from April to July. Data centers and
power are propelling current momentum. Though construction has also strengthened across sports

facilities and communication infrastructure, commercial projects remain stuck in low gear. Categories
like warehousing and retail remain soft, and overall architectural billings have improved only modestly.
Looking ahead, a recent breakout in megaprojects is likely to support additional data center and
manufacturing development, while higher interest rates limit overall growth.

Net Exports & Trade
• Net exports are tracking to slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth amid
strong imports.
Imports are shaping up for another strong quarter after a surge in July and a rebound in August
retail spending suggesting continued domestic demand for product. The July import gain was again

almost entirely driven by high-tech related goods like computers & accessories, semiconductors and
telecommunications equipment. We still anticipate a bit more of a broadening out in imports to come,
perhaps as early as August as businesses replenish inventory amid continued consumer demand.
Export growth is a bit more constrained, suggesting an expansion in the trade deficit and that net
exports will slice nearly two-percentage points off headline GDP growth in Q3.

Labor Market
• We modestly raised our 2026 payroll forecast and made no material changes to our 2027 forecast.
We continue to expect the unemployment rate to end this year at 4.2%.
August's employment report reinforced that the labor market remains stable. Payroll growth has
averaged 106K over the past six months, though we expect some moderation in the months ahead.
Hiring measures from regional Fed surveys and hiring plans from small businesses have cooled from

their early summer jumps and job openings have leveled off. At the same time, initial jobless claims
remain near multi-decade lows and continue to indicate that layoffs remain contained. We expect
payroll growth to moderate to roughly 65K per month over the remainder of the year, a pace broadly
consistent with our estimate of breakeven. As hiring cools slightly and the participation rate rebounds
somewhat further, we see some scope for the unemployment rate to edge up to 4.2% by year-end.

Even with the slight increase though, labor market conditions would remain consistent with full
employment.
Inflation
• Incorporating our expectations for the upcoming BEA methodology changes and recent inflation
data, we look for the core PCE deflator to end the year at 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis, up from
3.1% in our previous forecast.
Headline inflation remains tied to the conflict in the Middle East, although underlying price pressures
continue to move in a more favorable direction. Higher oil prices are keeping some upward pressure

on energy-sensitive categories in the near term, but tariff-related goods inflation is fading and price
pressures outside a handful of technology-related categories remain limited. Meanwhile, core services
inflation remains on a moderating trend as shelter inflation continues to gradually cool and labor cost
pressures slowly ease. Taken together, inflation pressures appear less widespread than earlier this year,
Economics | 5

### Page 6

Monthly Economics
but recurring supply shocks are likely to keep the path back to 2% bumpy. We expect the core PCE
deflator to slow to 3.2% on a Q4-over-Q4 basis this year.

Fiscal Policy
• We forecast this year's federal budget deficit will total $2.1 trillion (~6.5% of GDP), and next year's
will be about the same in dollar terms and a bit smaller as a share of GDP (~6%).

The federal budget deficit continues to hover in the 6.0-6.5% of GDP range, similar to where it was
at the end of the Biden administration. The outlook for the next couple of years is one of status quo.
We doubt there will be any new, big fiscal legislation with divided government likely after the midterm
elections, but rising interest costs and steady growth in mandatory spending programs such as Social
Security and Medicare will keep deficits from shrinking much. It's not just U.S. fiscal problems putting

upward pressure on long-term rates. Other factors such as robust corporate debt issuance, policy
uncertainty and foreign factors are also playing a role. Our base case for 6.0%-6.5% of GDP budget
deficits will not help when it comes to bringing down long-term interest rates.
6 | Economics

### Page 7

U.S. Economic Outlook: September 2026 Economics
Monetary Policy & Interest Rates
• We have changed our fed funds forecast to 4.00-4.25% by year-end 2026, with no changes in
2027. Our year-end forecasts for the 10-year Treasury yield are also higher at 4.90% and 4.75%,
respectively, for this year and next.
The FOMC made clear at its September 16 meeting that stubbornly high PCE inflation this year was
the key factor behind the Committee's decision to raise rates. Given this, we think only a string of

good inflation data can bail the Fed out, and accordingly we think one more rate hike is coming this
year, with softer inflation data in 2027 giving way to a more patient FOMC. We could easily envision
one more hike beyond that or a "one and done" hike where the FOMC does not go again. Outcomes
outside that range, however, strike us as unlikely. A rising neutral rate makes meaningful rate cuts hard
to justify in the absence of an economic slowdown, while the case for a more aggressive tightening

cycle seems weak given that the labor market is not overheating and much of the excess inflation is
supply side driven. We think a very short gradual tightening cycle and an eventual reduction in energy
prices/slowdown in inflation will allow Treasury yields to drift modestly lower in 2027. But, the factors
mentioned above (e.g., large budget deficits, rising neutral rate) should prevent yields from falling in a
bigger way.

International Developments
• Global growth has proved more resilient than expected, prompting us to upgrade our 2026 global
GDP growth forecast to 2.9%. We have also modestly revised our global inflation outlook higher,
lifting our 2026 CPI forecast to 4.4% and our 2027 forecast accordingly.
• We now expect more tightening across advanced economies than in our previous monthly forecast
update, while emerging market policy paths remain mixed.

Global economic activity proved more resilient than expected in H1-2026, with early indicators
suggesting that momentum carried into Q3. While precautionary stockpiling supported some of the
earlier strength, activity has remained resilient on firmer services, continued AI-related investment and
exports (U.S., South Korea, Japan and Australia) and increased defense spending (Eurozone and Japan).
We expect inflation to remain higher for longer before easing next year and averaging 4% in 2027 as

tighter financial conditions weigh on demand and supply pressures fade. Risks remain skewed to the
upside. Oil and product inventories remain below pre-conflict levels, while limited refining capacity and
ongoing disruptions leave energy prices vulnerable to renewed pressure. El Niño and higher fertilizer
costs threaten food prices, while AI-related demand is raising semiconductor costs. Second-round
effects remain limited, but successive supply shocks increase the risk of persistent inflation into next

year.

国。

称，伊朗和美国上周在纽约联合国⼤会期间举⾏的会谈进展甚微；伊⽅认为， 11 ⽉ 3 ⽇美中选结

伊朗要求美国解释：为何拒绝 “7 ⽇计划 ” ？
在美国公开拒绝伊朗⽅案后，伊朗⽅⾯仍在等待美⽅的正式说明。

常驻联合国代表华尔兹此前的表态来看，美⽅ “ 似乎还没有读过相关⽂件 ” ，因此要求美国解释为
何拒绝这⼀⽅案。

⽽华尔兹 27 ⽇接受采访时则称，伊朗提出的 “7 ⽇停⽕计划 ” 是 “ ⼀种相当愤世嫉俗的企图 ” 。美国
与伊朗对于⽅案内容以及双⽅各⾃所提出的条件，仍存在明显分歧。
路透社最新报道显⽰，阿拉格⻬ 27 ⽇曾表⽰，调解⽅尚未正式向伊朗传达美国拒绝⽅案的决
定，伊朗正在等待华盛顿的最终⽴场；他同时强调，伊朗⽅⾯的条件是明确的，霍尔⽊兹海峡
能否重新开放取决于这些条件是否得到满⾜。
伊朗最⾼领袖：伊朗现已变得独⽴、强⼤
在外交渠道继续运作的同时，伊朗最⾼领袖穆杰塔巴 · 哈梅内伊 28 ⽇再次强调伊朗⾃⾝的实⼒与

独⽴性。
新华社报道，穆杰塔巴 28 ⽇表⽰，伊朗的敌⼈企图恢复的那个充斥着西⽅列强对伊朗事务⼲涉
的时代已永远终结，伊朗现已变得独⽴、强⼤。
在黎巴嫩真主党前领导⼈哈桑 · 纳斯鲁拉去世两周年之际发表的书⾯致辞中，穆杰塔巴对⽐了
1979 年伊斯兰⾰命胜利初期与如今的伊朗，称伊朗过去仅拥有有限的⾃卫⼿段，⽽如今已经具
备对抗敌⼈的能⼒，在进步、独⽴以及在伊斯兰世界的地位⽅⾯均取得⻓⾜发展。
穆杰塔巴表⽰， “ 敌⼈渴望重现的过去时光已经⼀去不复返，绝不会再重演 ” 。他称，那些曾经企
图主宰伊朗的势⼒如今不得不正视伊朗的军事实⼒；与⼏⼗年前相⽐，伊朗的国防能⼒已经发
⽣根本性变化，并改变了⼒量对⽐。
他还表⽰，伊朗现已 “ 独⽴、强⼤ ” ，敌⼈希望伊朗回到过去的时代已经不会重演。 CBS 新闻援引
伊朗⽅⾯消息称，穆杰塔巴当天还表⽰，敌⽅⼒量未来将被逐出阿拉伯海。
穆杰塔巴的这番表态发布之际，美伊围绕霍尔⽊兹海峡的军事对峙仍在持续。此前阿拉格⻬也
表⽰，伊朗⼀⽅⾯已经为战事重燃做好准备，另⼀⽅⾯仍准备通过外交途径解决问题。

外交渠道出现新的接触之际，美伊军事对抗的⻛险并未消失。

击， 8 名海军陆战队⼈员受伤。

乘船只在霍尔⽊兹海峡遭伊朗巡航导弹攻击，他们因吸⼊烟雾受伤，并且出现头痛等关联脑震
荡的症状，因此可能还存在创伤性脑损伤。他们 “ 伤得不重 ” ，袭击发⽣后不久返回岗位。

Document 318: 「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」要請から3年の現在地（後編） ～投資家の評価と会社の行動からみる次の課題～

Source PDF: all report pdf/「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」要請から3年の現在地（後編） ～投資家の評価と会社の行動からみる次の課題～.pdf
Report source: DLRI
Date: 2026-09-17
Pages: 7
Status: ok

### Page 1

Research Report

2026 年 9 月 17 日

1/7 
「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」要請から 3 年の現在地（後編） 
～投資家の評価と会社の行動からみる次の課題～ 
 政策調査部 シニア研究員  河谷 善夫

（要旨） 
○ 前編では、東証の「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」要請後のPBR・ROE の変化を確認し、
改善が会社全体に一様に広がったわけではない一方、投資家から取組みの進展を評価された
会社では、とりわけ PBR 改善との対応が比較的明瞭であることを示した。後編では、投資家
が会社の何を評価しているのか、会社が投資家との対話をどのように経営行動に結びつけて
いるのかを分析する。 
○ まず、投資家から取組みの進展を評価された 295 社へのコメントを「資本配分」「事業ポー
トフォリオ改革」 「資本効率経営」 「経営陣のリーダーシップ」 「開示・対話の内容、姿勢」
の 5 カテゴリーに分類し、各カテゴリーの該当・非該当会社で PBR・ROE 改善率を比較した。 
○ その結果、事業ポートフォリオ改革やリーダーシップではPBR 改善率の差が比較的大きい一
方、資本配分や資本効率経営ではROE 改善率の差が大きいなど、評価された取組みによって
改善の現れ方に違いがみられた。開示・対話の内容、姿勢では単独での改善率差は小さく、
個別施策の効果を単純に切り分けることは難しい。 
○ 一方、投資家の評価は複数の取組みにまたがるケースが多い。例えば、事業ポートフォリオ
改革を評価された会社の63.2％は資本効率経営でも評価され、 資本配分を評価された会社の
66.0％も資本効率経営で評価されていた。また、単一カテゴリーのみの会社に比べ、複数カ
テゴリーで評価された会社では PBR・ROE 改善率が高い傾向もみられた。 
○ さらに会社側 682 社へのアンケートをみると、会社が認識する投資家の関心と実際の取組み
との関係は、PBR・ROE によるグループによって異なる。全体では、事業戦略・成長戦略や成
長分野への投資について、投資家の関心率が会社の実施率を大きく上回る一方、株主・投資
家との対話では会社の実施率が関心率を上回っていた。 
○ これらから、資本コストを意識し、開示や対話を行うこと自体にとどまらず、そこで得られ
た示唆を事業ポートフォリオ、資本配分、成長投資など具体的な経営判断に落とし込み、持
続的な収益性と市場評価の向上につなげることが次の課題と考えられる。「対話すること」
から「経営に実装すること」へ、取組みの実効性がより問われている。

### Page 2

2/7 
1. はじめに 
今回、河谷（2026）の分析枠組みを踏襲しつつ、対象をプライム市場からスタンダード市場ま
で広げた2,903 社について、 「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」の現在地を前後編の2 回にわた
って考察している（注1）。前編では、東証要請後の改善が市場全体に一様に広がったわけではな
い一方、投資家から取組みの進展を評価された会社では、とりわけPBR改善との対応が比較的明瞭
であることを示した。ただし、投資家からの評価とPBR改善との因果関係を示すものではない。 
後編となる本稿では、2026 年8 月に東証が公表した、投資家が取組みの進展を評価した会社リ
ストとその評価内容、ならびに会社向けアンケート調査結果を用いて分析を深める。投資家が何
を評価しているのか、会社が投資家との対話をどのように経営行動に結びつけているのかを考察
し、取組みの現在地と今後の課題を確認する。

2. 投資家は何を評価しているのか 
本章の分析では、投資家から取組みの進展を評価された各会社（295 社、注2）に関するコメン
トについて、生成AIを補助的に用いて内容を整理し、投資家の評価観点を「資本配分」 「事業ポー
トフォリオ改革」 「資本効率経営」 「経営陣のリーダーシップ」 「開示・対話の内容、姿勢」の5 つ
のカテゴリーに分類した（以下、本文及び図表では「開示・対話」と略記する。注3）。各カテゴ
リーに該当する会社と該当しない会社について、2023 年2 月末から2026 年 3 月末までのPBR・ROE
改善率を比較したものが資料1 である（注4）。

資料 1 各カテゴリーに該当する会社と該当しない会社のPBRとROEの改善率の比較 
<PBR>

<ROE>

（出所）東証公表資料及びBloombergデータより第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

### Page 3

3/7 
この比較で注目されるのは、投資家が評価した取組みによって、PBRとROEの改善の現れ方が異
なる点である。事業ポートフォリオ改革や経営陣のリーダーシップではPBR改善率の差が相対的に
大きい一方、資本配分や資本効率経営ではROE改善率の差が大きい。これに対し、開示・対話では
単独での改善率差は小さい。こうした結果からは、投資家が評価する取組みによって、収益性の
改善と市場からの再評価との対応の仕方が異なる傾向がうかがえる。 
もっとも、各会社の評価コメントには複数の取組みが同時に含まれており、この比較から個々
の施策の独立した効果を識別することはできない。そこで次章では、個別の取組みを切り分ける
のではなく、複数の取組みがどのように併せて評価されているかを確認する。

3. 投資家評価にみられる取組みの複合性 
資料 2 左は、各カテゴリーが他のカテゴリーとどの程度重複して評価されているかを示す共起
表である。

資料 2 5 つのカテゴリーの共起表と投資家評価カテゴリー数別PBR・ROEの改善率の比較 
<共起表>     <投資家評価カテゴリー数別PBR・ROEの改善率の比較>

（出所）資料 1 と同じ

例えば、事業ポートフォリオ改革について評価された会社68 社のうち、63.2％にあたる43 社
は資本効率経営についても評価されている。また、資本配分について評価されている188 社のう
ち、66.0％にあたる124 社は資本効率経営でも評価されているということがわかる。リーダーシ
ップが評価されている会社の60.4％が開示・対話でも評価されている。 
次に、単一カテゴリーのみで評価された会社に比べ、複数カテゴリーで評価された会社では、
PBR・ROEの改善率が高い傾向がみられる。また、獲得票数が1 票の会社より、2 票以上の会社の方
が改善率は高い（資料3）。

### Page 4

4/7 
資料 3 評価された投資家数(票数)別PBR・ROEの改善率の比較

（出所）資料 1 と同じ

これらの結果は、投資家が会社の取組みを単一の施策ではなく、複数の経営課題への対応の組
み合わせとして評価していることを示唆している。また、単一カテゴリーのみで評価された会社
より、複数カテゴリーで評価された会社の方がPBR・ROEの改善率は高い傾向にある。 
ただし、この結果を「評価される取組みの数が多いほどPBR・ROEが改善する」と直ちに解釈す
ることには注意が必要である。複数の投資家から評価された会社では、評価コメントに含まれる
論点も増え、結果として該当カテゴリー数が多くなる可能性があるためである。今回のデータだ
けでは、取組みの多様さと投資家からの評価の強さ（票数）の影響を切り分けることはできな
い。したがって、ここでは、複数の取組みを併せて評価された会社では、PBR・ROEの改善率が相
対的に高い傾向がみられると捉えるのが適切であろう。

4.  対話を企業価値向上につなげるための課題 
ここまでは投資家側から評価された会社の特徴をみてきた。次に視点を会社側に移し、東証が
実施した会社向けアンケートから、会社が認識する投資家の関心と実際の取組みとの関係を確認
する。アンケート回答会社は 682 社であり、個社名は示されていないものの、それぞれが属する
PBR・ROEのグループは示されている。第1 グループ 294 社、第2 グループ106 社、第3 グループ
215 社、第4 グループ67 社となっている。 
このアンケートでは、 「投資家の自社の取組みに対する関心事項」と「対話で得られた意見を踏
まえて実施した取組み」に共通する14 項目がある（注5） 。本稿では、そのうち、事業戦略、収益
性、成長投資、資本政策、対話という主要な経営課題をカバーする5 項目を取り上げる。 
資料 4 は、投資家がその事項に対して関心を持っていると認識した会社のうち、実際に同じ事
項について取組みを実施した会社の割合（対応率）である。

### Page 5

5/7 
資料 4 5 つの事項についての対応率の状況

（出所）東証公表データより第一ライフ資産運用経済研究所作成

例えば、低PBR・低ROE会社では「既存事業の収益性向上」の対応率が相対的に高い一方、高
PBR・高ROE会社では「成長分野への投資」の対応率が比較的高い。すべての項目で第1 グルー
プ会社の対応率が高いわけではなく、会社が置かれた状況によって投資家との対話を踏まえて
重点を置く取組みも異なっていることがうかがえる。 
次に、同じ5 つの事項について、投資家がその事項に関心を持った割合と、会社が実施した
割合の差（関心率－実施率）を示したのが資料5 である。

資料 5 5 つの事項についての投資家の関心率と会社の実施率の差

（出所）資料 4 と同じ

資料5 からは、全体では、 「事業戦略・成長戦略」で関心率が実施率を44.0％Pt、 「成長分野
への投資」でも35.5％Pt上回る。一方、 「株主・投資家との対話」は-19.2％Ptで、会社の実施
率が関心率を上回っている。これは、株主・投資家との対話自体には会社側が幅広く取り組ん
でいる一方、会社が認識する投資家の関心を踏まえ、事業戦略や成長投資など具体的な経営行
動につなげる余地はなお大きいことを示唆している。

5.  最後に～対話から実装へ 
本シリーズでは、東証が公表したデータを用いて、 「資本コストや株価を意識した経営」の現

### Page 6

6/7 
在地を確認してきた。前編では、東証要請後のPBR・ROEの変化を確認し、改善が会社全体に一
様に広がったわけではない一方、投資家から取組みの進展を評価された会社では、PBR・ROEの
改善も相対的に進んでいることを確認した。特にプライム市場上場会社に限定して比較して
も、PBR1 倍割れからの脱却との対応は比較的明瞭であった。 
後編ではさらに、投資家が評価する内容と会社側の対応をみた。投資家の評価は、資本配
分、事業ポートフォリオ改革、資本効率経営、経営陣のリーダーシップ、開示・対話など複数
の取組みにまたがっており、PBRとROEの改善の現れ方にも違いがみられた。 
一方、会社側アンケートからは、投資家との対話を踏まえた取組みがみられる一方、事業戦
略・成長戦略や成長分野への投資などでは、投資家の関心と会社の実施との間になおギャップ
があることも確認された。 
これらを踏まえると、東証の要請後の次の課題は、資本コストを意識して開示や投資家との
対話を行うことに加え、そこで得られた示唆を具体的な経営判断へ落とし込むことにあると考
えられる。事業ポートフォリオの見直し、資本配分、成長投資などを自社の経営課題に応じて
実行し、それらを持続的な収益性と市場評価の向上につなげられるか。 「対話すること」から
「経営に実装すること」へ、取組みの実効性がこれまで以上に問われるようになっているとい
えよう。

【注釈】 
1) 本稿の分析では、前編と同様、2023 年2 月末および2026 年 7 月末の双方で東証プライム市場
またはスタンダード市場に属する会社のうち、分析に必要なPBR・ROEデータを取得できた
2,903 社を対象としている。 
2) 前編で示したとおり、投資家により取組みの進展を評価された会社は300 社であるが、今回の
分析対象会社とした2,903 社に含まれるのは295 社である。 
3) 投資家が各会社について評価したコメントを生成AIに読み込ませ、コメントに共通してみられ
る論点や用語を基に評価観点を整理した。その上で、生成AIによる整理を参考に筆者が「資本
配分」 「事業ポートフォリオ改革」 「資本効率経営」 「経営陣のリーダーシップ」 「開示・対話の
内容、姿勢」の5 カテゴリーを設定し、各コメントについて該当するカテゴリーを分類した。
なお、一つのコメントが複数の論点を含む場合には複数カテゴリーへの該当を認め、生成AIに
よる判定が困難または曖昧なものについては、筆者が原文を確認して最終的に判断した。 
4) なお、各カテゴリーの該当・非該当間の改善率について二群の比率差検定を行ったところ、5％
水準で有意な差は確認されなかった。このため、以下では改善率の違いを傾向として捉えてい
る。 
5) この 2 つの質問に含まれる回答事項は「投資家の自社の取組みに対する関心事項」が15 個、 「対
話で得られた意見を踏まえて実施した取組み」が14 個である。前者の中の「短期的業績見通し」だ
けが後者の回答事項に含まれていない。その他はすべて対応している。

Document 319: 「関西の景気動向」９月号 AI関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産（PDF：841KB）

2026 年９月

１．景気の現状と展望 
 関西の景気は、緩やかな回復基調を維持しつつも、一部に弱い動きがみられる。企業部門では、円安傾向に
加えて、世界的なAI関連需要の拡大を背景に輸出額は増加している。AI関連投資の活発化などを背景に、
鉱工業生産にも持ち直しの動きがみられる。設備投資は、中東情勢の混乱などによる先行き不透明感が意識
されるなかでも、企業の収益計画が高めの水準を維持するもとで拡大基調が続いている。一方、家計部門では、
所得環境の改善が続くものの物価高が重石となり、個人消費の回復ペースは緩慢にとどまっている。 
先行きを展望すると、中東情勢の混乱が長引くなか、資源価格の高止まりが企業活動の重石となる。一方、
世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大が関西の生産や輸出に波及する動きが続き、設備投資の押し上げにもつながる
と見込まれる。個人消費は、持続的な賃上げの動きが下支えに寄与するものの、物価高による下押し圧力が
根強く、回復ペースは引き続き緩慢にとどまる見通しである。

２．個別指標の動き 
（１） 輸出・生産動向 
関西の輸出額（円ベース）は大きく増加している。国・地域別にみると、中国（香港を含む）向けや、アジ
ア（除く中国・香港）向けの伸びが大きく、ともに半導体等電子部品の増加がけん引している。米国向けは振
れを伴いつつも増加傾向にある。品目別にみると、電気機器では半導体等電子部品、一般機械では半導体
等製造装置の増加が顕著にみられ、世界的な AI 関連需要の高まりが押し上げに寄与しているとみられる。金
属及び同製品も先端材料としての需要増や、それに伴う市況の高騰を背景に高水準で推移している。 
一方、輸出を数量面でみると、回復傾向にあるものの、ペースは緩やかにとどまっている。中国向けやアジア
NIEs 向けは低水準での推移が続いているほか、ASEAN 向けも伸び悩んでいる。米国向けは振れを伴いつつ
も、基調としては底堅く推移している。日本銀行が公表している実質輸出指数にも増勢の加速はみられない。

以上のように、輸出数量の回復が緩慢にとどまる一方、輸出金額が大幅に増加しており、両者の動きに乖離
がみられる。これは、円安による輸出金額の押し上げや、AI 関連需要の拡大を受けた関連財の価格高騰によ
る影響が大きい。

鉱工業生産は、持ち直しの動きが続いている。業種別にみると、半導体製造装置やデータセンター向け設備
など AI 関連需要の裾野の広がりが追い風となっている生産用機械工業や電気・情報通信機械工業、機能性
素材や医薬品の増産を受けた化学工業の上振れが目立つ。一方、電子部品・デバイス工業は依然として低
水準での推移が続いている（関西における電子部品・デバイス生産の低迷について、詳しくは後述のトピックス
を参照）。

（２） 企業収益・設備投資動向 
企業収益を日本銀行大阪支店「短観」（６月調査、全規模・全産業）でみると、関西企業全体の 2026
年度の経常利益（全産業・全規模）は前年度比▲12.9％と、減益見通しとなった。中東情勢の混乱が長
引くなか資源価格の高止まりなどが収益の下押し圧力となっている。もっとも、2025 年度が同＋16.3％と、価
格転嫁の浸透などを背景に想定を大きく上回って着地したため、その反動減という側面も大きい。コロナ禍前と
の比較では、引き続き高めの水準を維持している。

企業が高水準の収益を見込むもとで、関西企業は積極的な設備投資姿勢を維持している。日本銀行大
阪支店「短観」６月調査による2026年度の設備投資計画（全産業・全規模）は前年度比＋9.7％と、過
去（2010～2024 年度）の平均伸び率を上回る水準にある。機械設備や建設資材の価格上昇などから名
目の投資額が押し上げられている点は割り引く必要があるものの、中東情勢の緊迫化が企業の設備投資マイ
ンドに与える影響は今のところ限定的にとどまっている。ソフトウエア投資も前年度比＋5.1％と増勢が続いてお
り、効率化や省力化を目的とした企業の積極的な投資姿勢に変化はみられない。

（３） 雇用・所得動向 
関西の雇用環境は、人手不足を背景に良好な状態が続いている。雇用関連指標をみると、有効求人倍率
は１倍を超え、完全失業率（季節調整値）は 2％台後半の低位で推移している。雇用者数は高めの水準
を保っている。 
所得面をみると、府県別「毎月勤労統計調査」から推計した４～６月期の関西の一人当たり現金給与総
額は前年同期比＋4.3％と、前期に比べてプラス幅が拡大した。今年の春闘における高い賃上げ水準での妥
結や、夏季賞与の増加（大阪府内企業を対象とする調査では前年比＋2.5％）などを反映し、賃金は高め

の伸びとなった。雇用者報酬を実質ベースでみると、物
価高による下押し圧力の緩和も相まって、4～６月期
は前年同期比＋３％超の増加となった。もっとも、春
先以降の原油高の影響がラグを伴って波及すると予想
されるほか、中東情勢の混乱が長期化するなか、原油
価格は依然として高水準にある。先行き、物価の伸び
は再び拡大する公算が大きく、実質所得には下押し圧
力が強い状況が続く見込みである。

（４） 消費動向 
関西の小売販売額をみると、スーパー、コンビニエンスストア、百貨店の販売額は緩やかに増加している。 
需要側統計（家計調査）をみると、実質ベースで見た消費支出は、4 月に税負担の軽減が実施された自
動車購入の増加などによる一時的な押し上げがみられたものの、均してみれば、物価高への警戒感が根強いも
とで、回復の勢いは鈍い。 
消費者マインドは、中東情勢の混乱直後の大幅
な悪化から改善傾向にあるものの、依然として悪化
前の水準を回復していない。とりわけ、大幅な落ち込
みがみられた「暮らし向き」や「耐久消費財の買い時
判断」の改善が遅れている。地政学リスクの長期化を
背景とした物価高への根強い警戒感が消費者マイン
ドの重石となっている。 
先行きの個人消費は、賃上げの持続が下支えに
寄与するものの、消費者物価の伸びが再び拡大する
もとで、回復ペースは緩慢にとどまる公算が大きい。

（５）住宅・公共投資動向 
関西の新設住宅着工戸数は、2025 年
４月の法改正を受けて上下に大きく振れた
ものの、 その後は落ち着きを取り戻し、概ね
法改正の影響を受ける前の水準に復してい
る。住宅需要は、金利の上昇や建築費の高
騰などによる下押し圧力が強いものの、将来
の資産価値への期待や、金利・建築費の先
高観を背景とした需要の高まりが下支えとな
り、大幅な落ち込みには至っていない。

2026 年７月の公共工事請負金額は前
年同月比＋15.0％となった。国土強靱化や
インフラ更新需要のほか、建設コストの上昇な
どを背景に、概ね前年を上回るペースで推移
している。

（６）倒産件数 
関西の企業倒産件数は、振れを伴いながらも、均してみるとコロナ禍前を上回る水準で推移している。原材
料費の高騰や人件費の上昇などに加え、金利上昇局面における資金繰りの悪化が懸念される。金利の上昇
によって新たに赤字に陥る中小企業の割合は全国よりも関西の方が大きいとの試算もあり、倒産件数は高止ま
りする可能性が高い。
